 \longrightarrow H^{2p}(A,\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{p,p}(A)$$ is surjective, where $\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^p(A)_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^p(A)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}$ and the intersection is taken in $H^{2p}(A,\mathbb{C})$. In particular, the rational Hodge conjecture holds in every codimension on every finite product of complex CM abelian varieties and on every power of such a product.*

</div>

The theorem also has consequences beyond the algebraicity of individual CM Hodge classes. The generalized Hodge conjecture predicts algebraic supports of the codimension allowed by the Hodge types of a cohomology substructure. We obtain it for CM abelian varieties by applying the theorem to correspondences from auxiliary CM varieties, following Hazama and Abdulali (Hazama 2003; Abdulali 2005) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Hazama2003CM](#ref-Hazama2003CM), [Abdulali2005CM](#ref-Abdulali2005CM)]. Two theorems of Milne then give the Tate conjecture for all abelian varieties over finite fields, which asks for algebraic representatives of Galois-invariant $\ell$-adic classes, and the Hodge standard conjecture for abelian varieties in arbitrary characteristic, which asserts positivity of the intersection form on primitive algebraic classes (Milne 1999, 2002) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Milne1999Tate](#ref-Milne1999Tate), [Milne2002Polarizations](#ref-Milne2002Polarizations)]. Section <a href="#cc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cc:section">8</a> states these conclusions with their coefficient and support conventions and explains the separate implications.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Hodge classes on CM abelian varieties

The codimension-one case of the Hodge conjecture is the Lefschetz $(1,1)$ theorem. Hodge formulated the broader problem by asking for an extension of this algebraicity criterion to higher-dimensional cycles (Hodge 1952, 184) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Hodge1952](#ref-Hodge1952)]. Cup products of divisor classes therefore give algebraic Hodge classes in higher degree. For an abelian variety, $H^*(A,\mathbb{Q})=\Lambda^*H^1(A,\mathbb{Q})$, but this description of its cohomology does not make every Hodge class a linear combination of divisor products. The problem is to construct cycles for the remaining classes.

Weil classes provide an important instance of this difficulty. Suppose an imaginary quadratic field $K$ acts on an abelian variety of dimension $2n$, with its two embeddings occurring with equal multiplicity in $H^{1,0}$. Then $\Lambda_K^{2n}H^1(A,\mathbb{Q})$ supplies a two-dimensional rational space of classes of type $(n,n)$. These determinant classes and their generalization to CM fields are discussed in (Deligne 1982, Proposition 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Deligne1982](#ref-Deligne1982)]. Their Hodge type follows from the multiplicity condition; algebraicity requires a geometric construction. Weil classes need not lie in the algebra generated by divisor classes; this occurs already on abelian fourfolds (Moonen and Zarhin 1999, Theorem 0.1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[MoonenZarhin1999](#ref-MoonenZarhin1999)]. Recent work of Markman proves algebraicity of the Weil classes on polarized abelian sixfolds of Weil type for an imaginary quadratic field, with polarization discriminant $-1$, and deduces the Hodge conjecture for all abelian fourfolds (Markman 2025, Theorem 1.5.1 and Corollary 1.6.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Markman2025Secant](#ref-Markman2025Secant)]. The sixfold theorem assumes an imaginary quadratic action, without requiring the full CM condition of Theorem <a href="#thm:cm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:cm">1</a>.

Deligne proved that every Hodge class on an abelian variety is *absolute Hodge*: after transport to a conjugate variety through de Rham cohomology, it remains a Hodge class (Deligne 1982, Main Theorem 2.11) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Deligne1982](#ref-Deligne1982)]. This establishes an arithmetic compatibility shared by algebraic cycle classes, but does not by itself produce algebraic cycles. CM varieties play a central role in that argument. Their Hodge groups are tori, and their degree-one cohomology splits, after extending coefficients, into lines indexed by field embeddings. An embedding-line tensor belongs to the scalar extension of the rational Hodge classes exactly when every scalar conjugate has equally many holomorphic and antiholomorphic factors. This is Pohlmann’s balance criterion (Pohlmann 1968) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Pohlmann1968](#ref-Pohlmann1968)]; see (Milne 2022, sec. 1.2(c)) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Milne2022](#ref-Milne2022)] and (Gao and Ullmo 2026, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[GaoUllmo2026](#ref-GaoUllmo2026)].

The CM arguments of Deligne and André reduce these Hodge classes to Weil-type classes on auxiliary CM abelian varieties (Deligne 1982; André 1992) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Deligne1982](#ref-Deligne1982), [Andre1992](#ref-Andre1992)]. More precisely, the formulation in (Milne 2022, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Milne2022](#ref-Milne2022)], attributed there to André, expresses a CM Hodge class as a sum of pullbacks of split Weil classes. Here *split* refers to the Hermitian polarization form having a totally isotropic subspace of half its dimension. This reduction identifies classes whose algebraicity would suffice; it does not supply their algebraic representatives in all dimensions.

There is also a reduction to relations involving only four labels. Hazama’s arrangement method spans its rational balance kernel by antipodal pairs and four-label square relations (Hazama 2003, Theorem 7.13) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Hazama2003CM](#ref-Hazama2003CM)]; his Corollary 7.14.1 gives the corresponding codimension-two algebraicity reduction. Gao and Ullmo give equivalent quadratic indicator relations and use them, after adjoining auxiliary CM factors, to generate Hodge-induced period relations (Gao and Ullmo 2026, Theorems 7.1–7.2) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[GaoUllmo2026](#ref-GaoUllmo2026)]. Their conclusion concerns relations among periods. Our task is to construct algebraic representatives for the corresponding four-factor tensors and to compose those representatives as correspondences. This distinction matters: the reduction uses auxiliary factors and polarization contractions, rather than asserting that low-codimension classes generate the Hodge ring of a fixed power.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### From balanced tensors to surface periods

Choose a Galois CM field $E$ containing the CM fields in $H^1(A,\mathbb{Q})$, and write $G_E=\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(E/\mathbb{Q})$ and $c$ for its complex conjugation. An odd sign function $v:G_E\to\{1,-1\}$, satisfying $v(c\tau)=-v(\tau)$, specifies a weight-one Hodge structure $U(v)$ on the rational vector space $E$. Its embedding line $U(v)_\lambda$, on which $a\in E$ acts as $\lambda(a)$, has type $(1,0)$ or $(0,1)$ according as $v(\lambda)$ is positive or negative. Section <a href="#sec:tensors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tensors">2</a> realizes $U(v)=H^1(B(v),\mathbb{Q})$ for a CM abelian variety $B(v)$.

The elementary relation needed in the proof is $$v_1+v_2=v_3+v_4.$$ It makes every scalar conjugate of the four-factor line $$U(v_1)_1\otimes U(v_2)_1\otimes U(v_3)_c\otimes U(v_4)_c
 \ \subset H^4\!\left(\prod_{i=1}^4B(v_i),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\right)$$ have type $(2,2)$. We prove that this line lies in the scalar extension of the rational cycle span. Section <a href="#sec:tensors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tensors">2</a> then assembles arbitrary balanced tensors from these relations: two-row switches connect lists with the same sum, and algebraic polarization pairings contract their intermediate factors. Transfer back to the original abelian variety and descent of coefficients give Theorem <a href="#thm:cm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:cm">1</a>.

The geometric construction of a four-factor class takes place on a surface. Suppose there are rational Hodge maps $h_i:U(v_i)\to H^1(S,\mathbb{Q})$, for a smooth projective surface $S$, and vectors $\alpha_i$ on the four selected embedding lines such that $$\int_S h_1(\alpha_1)\smile h_2(\alpha_2)
              \smile h_3(\alpha_3)\smile h_4(\alpha_4)\ne0.$$ Degree-one Hodge maps are induced, up to rational multiples, by morphisms $S\to B(v_i)$. The image of $S$ in their product therefore gives an algebraic cycle whose pairing with the desired line is nonzero. Section <a href="#sec:surface" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:surface">3</a> shows how four algebraic divisor kernels convert this functional into a vector on that exact line. The remaining problem is thus to find four degree-one classes with both a nonzero surface period and the prescribed rational CM sources.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Theta forms and their CM sources

Section <a href="#sec:theta" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:theta">4</a> constructs the period on a compact arithmetic quotient of the complex two-ball. Its unitary group has signature $(2,1)$ at one distinguished embedding and is definite at all the other real places. After the elementary normalization in Section <a href="#sec:tensors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tensors">2</a>, all four lines $U(v_i)_1$ are holomorphic at that embedding. To each type the construction assigns a family of holomorphic theta one-forms. Their values span a cotangent fiber, so two such families supply a wedge that is a nonzero holomorphic two-form.

To compare the two sides of the four-factor relation, we realize each pair of types by an orthogonal decomposition of the same Hermitian plane. The balance relation says that the two decompositions have the same local sign multiplicities. Weak approximation produces a rational second decomposition whose compact-place data are as close as desired to those of the first. At the distinguished embedding, the total period data depend only on the plane and remain unchanged.

A rank-two theta tensor packages the wedges into one total two-form. We keep its finite theta input and the compact quotient fixed while varying the compact-place data. The total form varies continuously, so its pairing with the initial nonzero form stays close to the nonzero self-pairing. At the two rational endpoints it is a finite sum of wedges of the required one-forms. Factoring these endpoints and passing to a common finite cover therefore gives a nonzero mixed period with two holomorphic and two antiholomorphic factors.

The construction uses Weil’s rational theta covariance and the unitary splittings of the Weil representation (Weil 1964; Kudla 1994; Borade et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Weil1964](#ref-Weil1964), [Kudla1994](#ref-Kudla1994), [BoradeEtAl2025ToriPeriods](#ref-BoradeEtAl2025ToriPeriods)]. Kudla and Millson developed differential-form theta kernels in this unitary setting (Kudla and Millson 1990, Introduction and Theorem 3) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[KudlaMillson1990DifferentialTheta](#ref-KudlaMillson1990DifferentialTheta)]. The projection turning the rank-two tensor into wedges has a close predecessor in the pure-cocycle product calculation of Bergeron, Millson, and Mœglin (Bergeron, Millson, and Mœglin 2016, Lemmas 5.7 and 5.11) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[BMM2016](#ref-BMM2016)]. We derive the required tensor identity in coordinates and use the fixed-input variation to obtain the mixed period.

These are initially complex one-form classes. The surface criterion also requires that they come from specified rational CM Hodge structures. Sections <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">5</a> and <a href="#sec:cm-sources" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cm-sources">6</a> establish this arithmetic input. First we realize the ball quotients in a projective moduli space and construct algebraic Hecke endomorphisms of the product of its components’ Albanese varieties. Counting ordinary isogeny neighbors gives a polynomial relation between these operators and Frobenius. We then compute the Hecke parameters of the theta forms. The Frobenius relation and the degree-one Weil weight force slope zero or one on the relevant Hecke summands, with the choice determined by the prescribed CM signs.

The Hecke projectors preserve the Hodge filtration, so weak admissibility turns these extreme slopes into pure Hodge types. Applying the calculation to every scalar conjugate identifies the complete CM type of each summand. Scalar descent then expresses every relevant theta class as a complex linear combination of images of the selected CM line under rational Hodge maps. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">7</a> puts the finite families on one projective surface, expands the nonzero period, and applies the surface criterion. This completes the four-factor construction and hence the proof of the main theorem.

<a id="sec:tensors"></a>
### CM tensors and four-factor relations

This section reduces Theorem <a href="#thm:cm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:cm">1</a> to the algebraicity of a specified family of tensors in the fourth cohomology of products of four CM abelian varieties. The reduction changes the CM types of individual factors while preserving their total Hodge type; its intermediate factors may therefore be absent from the original variety. We first record how to work with individual embedding lines and recover rationality after extending coefficients. We then construct the auxiliary CM varieties and assemble arbitrary balanced tensors from four-factor relations by algebraic correspondences.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Scalar coefficients and degree-one maps

For a smooth projective complex variety $X$, write $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hdg}}^{2p}(X)=H^{2p}(X,\mathbb{Q})\cap H^{p,p}(X),\qquad
 \mathcal Z^{2p}(X)=
 \mathop{\mathrm{im}}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^p(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}\longrightarrow H^{2p}(X,\mathbb{Q})\bigr).$$ Cohomology is Betti cohomology unless another theory is specified. We fix $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\mathbb{C}$, and write $T_k=T\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}k$ for a rational vector space $T$. A class with coefficients in $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}$ or $\mathbb{C}$ is called *algebraic* if it belongs to the corresponding scalar extension of $\mathcal Z^{2p}(X)$. Scalar conjugation therefore preserves algebraicity on the same complex variety: it fixes each rational cycle class and changes only its coefficient. We use the compatibility of cycle classes with exterior products, cup products, pullback between smooth varieties, and proper pushforward.

Our Tate convention is $V(r)^{a,b}=V^{a+r,b+r}$, so $V(r)$ has weight $\operatorname{wt}(V)-2r$.

<a id="stmt-lem-trace-descent"></a>
<div id="lem:trace-descent" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Scalar descent). *Let $T$ be a finite-dimensional rational vector space and $P\subset T_{\mathbb{C}}$ a complex-linear subspace. If $t\in T_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}$ and $\sigma t\in P$ for every $\sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})$, then $$t\in (T\cap P)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}.$$ The same assertion applies to the subspace of maps of any fixed Hodge type in a Hom space.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-trace-descent"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-trace-descent</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a finite Galois extension $K/\mathbb{Q}$ containing the coefficients of $t$. For $a\in K$, the vector $$T_a=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{K/\mathbb{Q}}(at)
     =\sum_{\sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(K/\mathbb{Q})}\sigma(a)\sigma(t)$$ is rational. Each automorphism of $K$ extends to $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}$, so every summand belongs to $P$; hence $T_a\in T\cap P$. If $b_1,\ldots,b_r$ is a rational basis of $K$ and $b_1^*,\ldots,b_r^*$ is its trace-dual basis, write $t=\sum_jt_jb_j$ with $t_j\in T$. Then $T_{b_j^*}=t_j$, which proves the assertion. The condition that a complex-linear map have a fixed Hodge type is complex-linear, so the same argument applies in a Hom space. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:cycle-descent"></a>
We will also use $$\label{eq:cycle-descent}
 \bigl(\mathcal Z^{2p}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\bigr)
       \cap H^{2p}(X,\mathbb{Q})=\mathcal Z^{2p}(X).$$ Indeed, extend a rational basis of $\mathcal Z^{2p}(X)$ to one of $H^{2p}(X,\mathbb{Q})$ and compare coefficients.

<a id="stmt-lem-weight-one-maps"></a>
<div id="lem:weight-one-maps" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Realization in degree one). *For complex abelian varieties $A$ and $B$, pullback induces an isomorphism $$\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(A,B)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
 \ \simeq\
 \mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{Hdg}}\bigl(H^1(B,\mathbb{Q}),H^1(A,\mathbb{Q})\bigr).$$ If $S$ is a smooth connected projective complex variety and $h:H^1(B,\mathbb{Q})\to H^1(S,\mathbb{Q})$ is a rational Hodge map, then there are an integer $m\geq1$ and a morphism $f:S\to B$ such that $f^*=mh$ on $H^1(B,\mathbb{Q})$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-weight-one-maps"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weight-one-maps</summary>

*Proof.* Dualizing a rational Hodge map gives a rational map on homology commuting with the complex structures. Multiplication by a positive integer makes it carry the integral lattice of $A$ into that of $B$. It consequently induces a holomorphic homomorphism of complex tori $A^{\mathrm{an}}\to B^{\mathrm{an}}$, which is algebraic. Conversely, an algebraic homomorphism induces the indicated Hodge pullback. This proves the first assertion; equivalently it is the dual of the rational form of Riemann’s theorem (Milne 2005, Proposition 6.5, Theorems 6.7–6.8, and Corollary 6.9) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[MilneShimura2017](#ref-MilneShimura2017)].

The Albanese map of $S$ induces an isomorphism $H^1(\mathop{\mathrm{Alb}}S,\mathbb{Q})\simeq H^1(S,\mathbb{Q})$. Apply the first assertion to the map with target $H^1(\mathop{\mathrm{Alb}}S,\mathbb{Q})$, clear a denominator, and compose the resulting homomorphism $\mathop{\mathrm{Alb}}S\to B$ with an Albanese map. ◻

</details>

The Lefschetz $(1,1)$ theorem says that $\mathop{\mathrm{Hdg}}^2(X)=\mathcal Z^2(X)$. In conjunction with Lemma <a href="#lem:trace-descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trace-descent">2</a>, it has the following useful consequence: if $t\in H^2(X,\overline{\mathbb{Q}})$ is of type $(1,1)$ in every scalar conjugate, then $t$ is algebraic. This observation will supply all two-factor tensors used below.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Rank-one CM structures

For a polarizable rational Hodge structure $H$ of weight one, let $J(H)$ be the smallest rational algebraic subgroup of $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}(H)$ containing the Hodge circle. The circle acts by $z$ on $H^{1,0}$ and by $\bar z$ on $H^{0,1}$, for $|z|=1$. It preserves an alternating polarization. Its connectedness shows that it lies in the rational identity component of $J(H)$; minimality therefore makes $J(H)$ connected.

<a id="stmt-lem-toric-cm"></a>
<div id="lem:toric-cm" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *For a complex abelian variety $A$, the following conditions are equivalent:*

1.  *$J(H^1(A,\mathbb{Q}))$ is a torus.*

2.  *$H^1(A,\mathbb{Q})$ is a direct sum of rational Hodge structures, each of rank one over a CM field acting by Hodge endomorphisms.*

3.  *$\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(A)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}$ contains a commutative semisimple algebra of rational dimension $2\dim A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-toric-cm"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-toric-cm</summary>

*Proof.* The zero-dimensional case is immediate. Put $H=H^1(A,\mathbb{Q})$, and first assume that $J(H)$ is a torus. Its associative envelope $E_0\subset\mathop{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H)$ is a commutative semisimple algebra: after splitting the torus, its elements act diagonally by scalars on the weight spaces, so the envelope is a subalgebra of a product of fields and is reduced. This description descends to $\mathbb{Q}$.

Let $*$ be the polarization adjoint. It preserves $E_0$, since it sends every symplectic torus element to its inverse. Every element of $E_0$ commutes with the complex structure $I$ supplied by the Hodge circle. Consequently $*$ is also the adjoint for the positive definite real form obtained by inserting $I$ in the polarization. In particular, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{H_{\mathbb{R}}}(a^*a)>0\qquad(0\ne a\in E_0\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}).$$ The involution cannot exchange two primitive factors of the real finite étale algebra $E_0\otimes\mathbb{R}$: for the idempotent $e$ of one factor, this would give $e^*e=0$. On a complex factor it cannot be the identity, since for its idempotent $e$ one would have $(ie)^*(ie)=-e$, of negative trace. Thus it acts as complex conjugation on each complex factor. There are no real factors. On the nonzero module of such a factor the circle would act by real scalars, and a continuous homomorphism from the circle to $\mathbb{R}^\times$ is trivial; this contradicts the weight-one characters.

It follows that each rational field factor $K$ of $E_0$ is CM. Indeed, it has only complex archimedean factors, and its involution satisfies $\tau(a^*)=\overline{\tau(a)}$ at every embedding. Its fixed field is therefore totally real and has index two. On the corresponding $K$-module the circle acts by $K\otimes\mathbb{R}$-scalars. Splitting that module into $K$-lines thus gives rational Hodge substructures. This proves (ii), without discarding multiplicities.

The separate scalar actions on the field lines in (ii) form a commutative semisimple Hodge-endomorphism algebra of dimension $\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H$. Lemma <a href="#lem:weight-one-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weight-one-maps">3</a> realizes it in $\mathop{\mathrm{End}}(A)\otimes\mathbb{Q}$, proving (iii). Conversely, write an algebra as in (iii) as $\prod_jK_j$. Its faithful action on $H$ has a positive rank $m_j$ over each $K_j$, and $$\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}H=\sum_jm_j[K_j:\mathbb{Q}]=\sum_j[K_j:\mathbb{Q}].$$ Hence every $m_j=1$. The centralizer of this action in $\mathop{\mathrm{GL}}(H)$ is the torus $\prod_j\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{K_j/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbf G_m$. It contains the Hodge circle because the algebra acts by Hodge endomorphisms, proving (i). ◻

</details>

These equivalences give the rank-one description of the CM varieties defined in the introduction. A finite product of CM abelian varieties is CM: the circle on the direct sum of their degree-one cohomologies lies in the product of their tori, and its connected rational envelope is a subtorus.

Choose a Galois CM field $E\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}$, and put $$F=E^+,\qquad G_E=\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(E/\mathbb{Q}),\qquad s=[F:\mathbb{Q}].$$ Its complex conjugation $c\in G_E$ is central. One such $E$ can contain any finite collection of CM fields and all their conjugates. To see this, take the compositum of all those conjugates. For a CM field $K$, every embedding $\rho:K\to\mathbb{C}$ satisfies $\rho c_K=c_{\mathbb{C}}\rho$. These identities show that complex conjugation on the Galois compositum commutes with its Galois group. Every conjugate of an element fixed by it is real, so the fixed field is totally real. The compositum is therefore a Galois CM field.

An *odd sign function* on $G_E$ is a function $v:G_E\to\{1,-1\}$ satisfying $v(c\tau)=-v(\tau)$. Give the rational vector space $E$ a weight-one Hodge structure $U(v)$ by declaring its embedding line $$U(v)_\lambda=
 \{u\in U(v)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}: au=\lambda(a)u\text{ for every }a\in E\}
 \qquad(\lambda\in G_E)$$ to have type $(1,0)$ if $v(\lambda)=1$, and type $(0,1)$ otherwise. Oddness makes these declarations compatible with the real structure.

<a id="eq:cm-types"></a>
This Hodge structure is polarizable. Choose $0\ne\delta\in E$ with $c(\delta)=-\delta$. Multiplying $\delta$ by an element of $F^\times$, weak approximation gives an element $\xi\in E^\times$ such that $c(\xi)=-\xi$ and $$\operatorname{sgn}\bigl(\operatorname{Im}\tau(\xi)\bigr)=-v(\tau)$$ at one representative $\tau$ of each conjugate pair. The form $$\psi_v(x,y)=\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{E/\mathbb{Q}}\bigl(\xi x c(y)\bigr)$$ is alternating and nondegenerate. It pairs only conjugate embedding lines. On a vector $u$ in the sum of the $(1,0)$ lines, $i\psi_v(u,\bar u)$ is a sum of positive multiples of the squared absolute values of its embedding coordinates; hence it is positive for $u\ne0$. This is the weight-one polarization condition. After scaling, the form is integral on a lattice. The Riemann criterion, applied to the dual homological Hodge structure, gives a CM abelian variety $B(v)$ of dimension $s$ with $$\label{eq:cm-types}
 U(v)=H^1(B(v),\mathbb{Q}).$$ This construction permits every odd sign function, including imprimitive CM types; it does not require a principal polarization. The polarization and Riemann-criterion argument also applies to any CM field, without a Galois hypothesis, and any CM type, that is, any choice of one embedding from each conjugate pair. The Galois field $E$ provides a common indexing set for the types used below.

<a id="eq:cm-label-conjugation"></a>
We will need the exact action of scalar conjugation on labels. If $\sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})$ restricts to $g\in G_E$ and $u\in U(v)_\lambda$, rationality of the $E$-action gives $$a\,\sigma(u)=\sigma(au)
             =\sigma(\lambda(a))\sigma(u)
             =(g\lambda)(a)\sigma(u).$$ Consequently $$\label{eq:cm-label-conjugation}
 \sigma U(v)_\lambda=U(v)_{g\lambda}.$$ In particular the complete type signs of $U(v)_1$ and $U(v)_c$ after $g$ are $v(g)$ and $-v(g)$, respectively. Here and below the commutation of $c$ with $g$ takes place in $G_E$; no centrality assertion is made in $\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})$.

The same convention applies to a line in a rank-one module over a CM subfield of $E$. Choose $\lambda\in G_E$ extending the embedding that labels the line. Its *complete type function* takes the value $+1$ at $g\in G_E$ if the line labeled by the restriction of $g\lambda$ has type $(1,0)$, and $-1$ if it has type $(0,1)$. It is odd. Since the embedding-line decomposition is defined over $E$, this is the type of the scalar-conjugate line for every automorphism of $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}$ restricting to $g$.

<a id="stmt-lem-cm-line-transfer"></a>
<div id="lem:cm-line-transfer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Transfer of an embedding line). *Let $H$ and $H'$ be direct sums of rank-one weight-one Hodge modules over CM fields contained in $E$. Choose an embedding line $L\subset H_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}$ and an embedding line $M\subset H'_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}$ with the same complete type function. Extend any $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}$-linear map $T:L\to M$ by zero on all other source embedding lines. Then $$T\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{Hdg}}(H,H')\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}.$$ When $H$ and $H'$ are degree-one cohomologies of abelian varieties, this map is therefore an algebraic-coefficient linear combination of pullbacks of abelian morphisms, up to rational denominators.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cm-line-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cm-line-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* Scalar conjugation transports the entire source and target decompositions. For each $\sigma$, the only possibly nonzero component of $\sigma T$ maps $\sigma L$ to $\sigma M$, whose types agree by hypothesis. All other components are zero. Thus $\sigma T$ is a Hodge map on the whole source. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:trace-descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:trace-descent">2</a> in $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(H,H')$, followed, when applicable, by Lemma <a href="#lem:weight-one-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weight-one-maps">3</a>. ◻

</details>

The map $T$ in this lemma need not be invariant under a stabilizer of either line. This matters when a field is a proper subfield of $E$, or when a rational field module occurs more than once. The proof treats every scalar conjugate of the zero-extended map and retains the repeated rational summands separately.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Reduction to four factors

The four-factor assertion that remains to be proved is the following.

<a id="stmt-thm-switch"></a>
<div id="thm:switch" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:switch-line"></a>
**Theorem 6** (Four-factor switch). *Let $E$ be a Galois CM field and let $v_1,v_2,v_3,v_4:G_E\to\{1,-1\}$ be odd sign functions satisfying $v_1+v_2=v_3+v_4$. Under the Künneth inclusion, the following line is algebraic: $$\label{eq:switch-line}
 U(v_1)_1\otimes U(v_2)_1\otimes U(v_3)_c\otimes U(v_4)_c
 \ \subset\
 H^4\!\left(\prod_{i=1}^4B(v_i),\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\right)$$*

</div>

This line is already of Hodge type $(2,2)$ in every scalar conjugate. Indeed, after an automorphism restricting to $g\in G_E$, its four type signs have sum <a id="eq:switch-conjugate-balance"></a>$$\label{eq:switch-conjugate-balance}
 v_1(g)+v_2(g)-v_3(g)-v_4(g)=0.$$ The theorem asks for algebraicity, which does not follow from this balance alone. We leave its proof to the subsequent construction; the present section proves that it is sufficient.

Hazama’s coordinate-arrangement calculation rationally spans the kernel of its Hodge matrix by antipodal pairs and elementary four-label relations (Hazama 2003, Theorem 7.13) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Hazama2003CM](#ref-Hazama2003CM)]. His Corollary 7.14.1 states a corresponding codimension-two algebraicity reduction. We prove the particular tensor reduction needed here directly, including the transfers to auxiliary abelian factors and the algebraic contractions that assemble the relations.

<a id="stmt-prop-switch-reduction"></a>
<div id="prop:switch-reduction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7**. *If Theorem <a href="#thm:switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:switch">6</a> holds for every Galois CM field and every quadruple of odd sign functions satisfying its relation, then Theorem <a href="#thm:cm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:cm">1</a> holds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-switch-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-switch-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* Let $A$ be a CM abelian variety and let $h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hdg}}^{2p}(A)$. The cases $p=0$ and $p=1$ follow from the unit class and the Lefschetz $(1,1)$ theorem, respectively. For higher codimension, we first replace a Hodge monomial by a tensor on auxiliary CM factors. We then connect its two lists of types by four-factor switches and compose the resulting algebraic tensors.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:toric-cm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:toric-cm">4</a>, choose a rational decomposition of $H=H^1(A,\mathbb{Q})$ into rank-one CM-field modules, retaining separate copies of repeated summands. Choose a Galois CM field $E$ containing all the fields and their conjugates. Over $E$, $H$ is the direct sum of its embedding lines. Since cup product gives $H^*(A,\mathbb{Q})=\Lambda^*H$, the exterior monomials in these lines give a direct-sum decomposition of $H^*(A,\overline{\mathbb{Q}})$ into one-dimensional lines (Milne 2005, Proposition 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[MilneShimura2017](#ref-MilneShimura2017)]. Every such line is contained in one Hodge bidegree.

Scalar conjugation permutes the monomial lines, with only the possible sign from reordering exterior factors. Because $h$ is rational, a nonzero component on one monomial line gives a nonzero component on every line in its scalar-Galois orbit. All these lines must have type $(p,p)$. The decomposition is defined over $E$, so the components of $h$ have algebraic Betti coefficients.

Fix a supported monomial and list the complete type functions of its $2p$ factors as $z_1,\ldots,z_{2p}$. Each $z_i$ is odd. In the conjugate indexed by $g$, the number of $(1,0)$ factors minus the number of $(0,1)$ factors is $\sum_i z_i(g)$. Its balance is therefore exactly $$\sum_{i=1}^{2p}z_i=0.$$ For $1\leq i\leq p$, set $x_i=z_i$ and $y_i=-z_{p+i}$. Then $\sum_i x_i=\sum_i y_i$. The first $p$ factors have the complete types of $U(x_i)_1$, and the last $p$ have those of $U(y_i)_c$. For any ordered list $a=(a_1,\ldots,a_p)$ of odd sign functions, put $A_a=\prod_{i=1}^pB(a_i)$; for two such lists put $$\mathscr L_{a,b}=
 \bigotimes_{i=1}^pU(a_i)_1\ \otimes\
 \bigotimes_{i=1}^pU(b_i)_c
 \subset H^{2p}(A_a\times A_b,\overline{\mathbb{Q}}).$$ Repeated factors in these products are kept as separate slots. Lemma <a href="#lem:cm-line-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cm-line-transfer">5</a> reduces the chosen monomial to the algebraicity of $\mathscr L_{x,y}$. We return to $A$ after composing the transition tensors.

Regard each list as a matrix with $p$ rows and one column for each representative of $G_E/\langle c\rangle$. At any column, equality of the two sums is equality of the numbers of plus signs. Among the rows where the current $x$-entry differs from the desired $y$-entry, there are equally many mismatches of the forms $(+1,-1)$ and $(-1,+1)$. Pair these two sets of rows and swap the two current entries in every pair, making the opposite swap at the conjugate coordinate. This corrects the column, leaves the other columns unchanged, and preserves oddness of every row. Successively correcting the columns gives a finite sequence $$x=x^{(0)},\ x^{(1)},\ \ldots,\ x^{(N)}=y$$ in which consecutive lists differ by one such two-row swap.

At a single swap the old rows $a,b$ and new rows $a',b'$ satisfy $a+b=a'+b'$ at every coordinate. Theorem <a href="#thm:switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:switch">6</a> supplies the pure algebraic tensor $$U(a)_1\otimes U(b)_1\otimes U(a')_c\otimes U(b')_c.$$ Every unchanged row is joined to its new copy by $U(v)_1\otimes U(v)_c$, an all-conjugate $(1,1)$ line and hence an algebraic two-factor tensor. Pull these classes to their distinct old and new slots and multiply. This gives a nonzero algebraic generator of $\mathscr L_{x^{(j)},x^{(j+1)}}$. If the original lists already agree, the two-factor tensors alone generate $\mathscr L_{x,x}$. It remains to compose consecutive transition tensors without losing their algebraicity or nonvanishing.

<a id="eq:transition-composition"></a>
For three ordered lists $a,b,e$, suppose that $\mathscr L_{a,b}$ and $\mathscr L_{b,e}$ have pure algebraic generators with algebraic Betti coefficients. Write them as $$\begin{split}
 t_{ab}&=\alpha_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_p\otimes
          \beta_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\beta_p,\\
 t_{be}&=\gamma_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\gamma_p\otimes
          \delta_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_p,
 \end{split}$$ where $\beta_i\in U(b_i)_c$ and $\gamma_i\in U(b_i)_1$. Identify the middle copies and choose an ample divisor class $\ell_i$ on each $B(b_i)$. With $r_{ab},r_{be},r_{ae}$ denoting the projections from $A_a\times A_b\times A_e$, and with the $\ell_i$ also denoting their pullbacks, cup product and pushforward give $$\label{eq:transition-composition}
 \begin{split}
 (r_{ae})_*\left(
 r_{ab}^*t_{ab}\smile r_{be}^*t_{be}
       \smile\prod_{i=1}^p\ell_i^{\,s-1}\right)
 &=
 (-1)^{p(p-1)/2}\left(\prod_{i=1}^p q_i\right)t_{ae},\\
 q_i&=\int_{B(b_i)}\beta_i\wedge\gamma_i\wedge\ell_i^{\,s-1},
 \end{split}$$ where $t_{ae}=\alpha_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_p\otimes
\delta_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\delta_p$. The sign is obtained by regrouping $\beta_1\cdots\beta_p\gamma_1\cdots\gamma_p$ into $\beta_1\gamma_1\cdots\beta_p\gamma_p$ in the indicated slot order.

Each $q_i$ is nonzero. Complex conjugation exchanges the two middle embedding lines, so $\gamma_i$ is a nonzero scalar multiple of $\bar\beta_i$. Hodge–Riemann positivity for the ample class makes the pairing with $\bar\beta_i$ nonzero, whether $\beta_i$ is holomorphic or antiholomorphic. This requires no compatibility between $\ell_i$ and $E$. Moreover $q_i\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^\times$, since its vectors have algebraic Betti coefficients and $\ell_i$ is rational. The codimension on the left of Equation <a href="#eq:transition-composition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transition-composition">[eq:transition-composition]</a> is $$p+p+p(s-1)-ps=p.$$ Thus it is an algebraic class of the required degree, and division by the nonzero algebraic coefficient shows that the endpoint line is algebraic. Iterating along the sequence of lists proves algebraicity of $\mathscr L_{x,y}$.

Finally choose the line transfers in the direction from the auxiliary $U$’s to $H^1(A,\mathbb{Q})$. Each is an algebraic-coefficient linear combination of rational pullbacks along maps from $A$ to the corresponding $B(v)$. Apply their exterior product on $A^{2p}$ and pull back along the diagonal $A\to A^{2p}$. The result is the cup product of the selected original factors. They occupy distinct embedding or multiplicity lines in the original exterior monomial, so this cup product is nonzero. We have proved that every supported monomial line is algebraic. Summing the components of $h$ and using Equation <a href="#eq:cycle-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cycle-descent">[eq:cycle-descent]</a> proves the rational assertion.

Finite products are covered because they are themselves CM, as observed after Lemma <a href="#lem:toric-cm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:toric-cm">4</a>. ◻

</details>

The proof uses tensors on auxiliary products and contracts their intermediate slots before returning to $A$. It does not assert generation of the Hodge ring on a fixed power by cup products of classes in codimensions one and two.

<a id="stmt-lem-switch-normalization"></a>
<div id="lem:switch-normalization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Choice of the distinguished embedding). *It suffices to prove Theorem <a href="#thm:switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:switch">6</a> when $s\geq2$ and $v_i(1)=1$ for all four functions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-switch-normalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-switch-normalization</summary>

*Proof.* If $v_1$ and $v_2$ are nowhere simultaneously positive, oddness also rules out simultaneous negativity. Hence $v_2=-v_1$. The relation gives $v_4=-v_3$, and the first two and the last two slots of Equation <a href="#eq:switch-line" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-line">[eq:switch-line]</a> are separately all-conjugate $(1,1)$ pairs.

Otherwise choose $g\in G_E$ at which $v_1(g)=v_2(g)=1$. The relation makes all four signs positive there. Choose a scalar automorphism extending $g$. It sends the labels in Equation <a href="#eq:switch-line" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-line">[eq:switch-line]</a> to $g,g,gc,gc$. The complete type function of the line labeled $g$ is the right translate $$v_i^g(\tau)=v_i(\tau g).$$ It is odd, satisfies $v_i^g(1)=1$, and the four translated functions satisfy the same relation. Centrality of $c$ in $G_E$ makes the complete type of the $gc$-line equal to $-v_i^g$. The transfers of Lemma <a href="#lem:cm-line-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cm-line-transfer">5</a> therefore carry the normalized switch for the $v_i^g$’s to the lines labeled $g,g,gc,gc$ on the original factors. Applying the inverse scalar automorphism gives Equation <a href="#eq:switch-line" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-line">[eq:switch-line]</a>.

When $s=1$, there is only one odd sign function positive at the identity, so the normalized tensor is a product of ordinary pairs. ◻

</details>

For the remaining construction we work in the normalized case, so all four lines $U(v_i)_1$ are holomorphic. The next section isolates a surface-period condition sufficient to prove the switch.

<a id="sec:surface"></a>
### A surface criterion for the switch

To prove Theorem <a href="#thm:switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:switch">6</a>, we will construct four degree-one classes on one surface with a nonzero cup product. The criterion below explains why this is sufficient. The four maps give an algebraic surface class that tests the switch line nontrivially; four divisor classes then convert that test into a vector on the line itself.

<a id="stmt-prop-surface-switch"></a>
<div id="prop:surface-switch" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:surface-period"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Surface criterion). *Let $E$ be a Galois CM field with conjugation $c$, and let $v_1,v_2,v_3,v_4:G_E\to\{1,-1\}$ be odd sign functions satisfying $v_1+v_2=v_3+v_4$. Put $\lambda=(1,1,c,c)$. Suppose that $S$ is a smooth connected projective complex surface and that there are rational Hodge maps $$h_i:U(v_i)\longrightarrow H^1(S,\mathbb{Q}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq4,$$ and nonzero vectors $\alpha_i\in U(v_i)_{\lambda_i}\subset U(v_i)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}$ such that $$\label{eq:surface-period}
 \int_S h_1(\alpha_1)\smile h_2(\alpha_2)
             \smile h_3(\alpha_3)\smile h_4(\alpha_4)\ne0.$$ Here each $h_i$ is extended to $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}$-coefficients. Then the line in Equation <a href="#eq:switch-line" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-line">[eq:switch-line]</a> is algebraic.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-surface-switch"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-surface-switch</summary>

*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#lem:weight-one-maps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weight-one-maps">3</a>, there are positive integers $m_i$ and morphisms $f_i:S\to B(v_i)$ with $f_i^*=m_i h_i$. Therefore <a id="eq:surface-morphism-period"></a>$$\label{eq:surface-morphism-period}
 k:=\int_S f_1^*\alpha_1\smile f_2^*\alpha_2
              \smile f_3^*\alpha_3\smile f_4^*\alpha_4
 \in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^\times.$$ Nonvanishing follows from Equation <a href="#eq:surface-period" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:surface-period">[eq:surface-period]</a>, since the new value is multiplied by $\prod_i m_i$. The value is algebraic because the pullbacks, cup product, and evaluation on $[S]$ are rational operations on Betti cohomology and the $\alpha_i$ have algebraic Betti coefficients.

Put $A=\prod_iB(v_i)$, $n=\dim A=4s$, and $f=(f_i):S\to A$. Let $$z=f_*[S]\in\mathop{\mathrm{CH}}^{n-2}(A),\qquad
 Z=\operatorname{cl}(z)\in\mathcal Z^{2n-4}(A).$$ For $t_\alpha=\alpha_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_4$ in the four-$H^1$ Künneth summand of $H^4(A,\overline{\mathbb{Q}})$, the projection formula gives <a id="eq:surface-functional"></a>$$\label{eq:surface-functional}
 \int_A Z\smile t_\alpha=k.$$ Thus $Z$ gives a nonzero functional on the switch line; it is not itself a vector on that line. In particular $f(S)$ cannot have dimension less than two, since then the proper pushforward of the two-cycle $[S]$ and the displayed pairing would be zero.

<a id="eq:surface-divisor-kernels"></a>
For each $i$, take a copy $B(v_i)'$ with its transported $E$-action, and choose a nonzero vector $\beta_i\in U(v_i)'_{c\lambda_i}$ with algebraic Betti coefficients. Under the Künneth inclusion define the pure class $$D_i=\alpha_i\otimes\beta_i
 \in H^2\bigl(B(v_i)\times B(v_i)',\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\bigr).$$ If $\sigma\in\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q})$ restricts to $g\in G_E$, then Equation <a href="#eq:cm-label-conjugation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cm-label-conjugation">[eq:cm-label-conjugation]</a> puts its two factors on the lines labeled $$g\lambda_i,\qquad gc\lambda_i=cg\lambda_i.$$ These have opposite Hodge types. This uses centrality of $c$ in $G_E$ only, and remains valid when the scalar coordinates of $\alpha_i$ and $\beta_i$ lie outside $E$. Every scalar conjugate of $D_i$ therefore has type $(1,1)$. Scalar descent and the Lefschetz $(1,1)$ theorem give $$\label{eq:surface-divisor-kernels}
 D_i\in
 \mathcal Z^2\bigl(B(v_i)\times B(v_i)'\bigr)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\overline{\mathbb{Q}}.$$ The entire cohomology class $D_i$ is algebraic here. It was defined in the pure $H^1\otimes H^1$ summand before applying descent, so there is no need to project an arbitrary divisor class onto that summand.

<a id="eq:surface-to-tensor"></a>
Set $A'=\prod_iB(v_i)'$. Let $p:A\times A'\to A$ and $p':A\times A'\to A'$ be the projections, and let $q_i:A\times A'\to B(v_i)\times B(v_i)'$ select the $i$-th old and new slots. Write $$K=\prod_{i=1}^4q_i^*D_i,\qquad
 t_\beta=\beta_1\otimes\cdots\otimes\beta_4.$$ Order the old factors first and the new factors second in the Künneth identification for $A\times A'$. Moving the new degree-one factor of $D_i$ past the later old degree-one factors makes $3+2+1=6$ sign changes. Hence $$K=(-1)^6\,t_\alpha\otimes t_\beta=t_\alpha\otimes t_\beta.$$ In particular $K$ has degree four in the old factors and degree four in the new factors. Integration over $A$ consequently gives the exact formula $$\label{eq:surface-to-tensor}
 p'_*\bigl(p^*Z\smile K\bigr)
   =\left(\int_A Z\smile t_\alpha\right)t_\beta
   =k\,t_\beta.$$ Any other Künneth component of $Z$ contributes zero to this integration. The codimension of the left side is $(n-2)+4-n=2$, equivalently its cohomological degree is $(2n-4)+8-2n=4$.

By Equation <a href="#eq:surface-divisor-kernels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:surface-divisor-kernels">[eq:surface-divisor-kernels]</a>, the left side of Equation <a href="#eq:surface-to-tensor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:surface-to-tensor">[eq:surface-to-tensor]</a> is algebraic with $\overline{\mathbb{Q}}$-coefficients. Since $k\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^\times$, division by $k$ proves algebraicity of the line generated by $t_\beta$, whose labels are $(c,c,1,1)$. Ordinary scalar complex conjugation fixes the rational cycle span on the same $A'$ and changes these labels to $(1,1,c,c)$. Identifying the copied factors with the original ones gives the asserted line on $A$. ◻

</details>

The theta construction produces complex classes, and the arithmetic argument will place them in spans of rational Hodge images. The following consequence applies to those classes.

<a id="stmt-cor-surface-span"></a>
<div id="cor:surface-span" class="corollary">

**Corollary 10** (Surface criterion for complex spans). *Let $E$, $v_1,\ldots,v_4$, and $\lambda=(1,1,c,c)$ be as in Proposition <a href="#prop:surface-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:surface-switch">9</a>, and let $S$ be a smooth connected projective complex surface. Suppose that, for each $1\leq i\leq4$, a class $\eta_i\in H^1(S,\mathbb{C})$ belongs to $$\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{C}}
 \{h(U(v_i)_{\lambda_i}):
       h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathrm{Hdg}}(U(v_i),H^1(S,\mathbb{Q}))\}$$ and that $$\int_S\eta_1\smile\eta_2\smile\eta_3\smile\eta_4\ne0.$$ Then the line in Equation <a href="#eq:switch-line" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:switch-line">[eq:switch-line]</a> is algebraic.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-surface-span"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-surface-span</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a nonzero algebraic Betti generator of each selected line. Each $\eta_i$ is a finite complex linear combination of its images under rational Hodge maps. Expanding the nonzero cup product selects a nonzero term of the form in Equation <a href="#eq:surface-period" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:surface-period">[eq:surface-period]</a>. Thus Proposition <a href="#prop:surface-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:surface-switch">9</a> applies. This argument uses only finite sums; all four maps have the same target $H^1(S,\mathbb{Q})$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:theta"></a>
### Theta differentials and a nonzero mixed period

The surface criterion of Proposition <a href="#prop:surface-switch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:surface-switch">9</a> asks for a nonzero period made from four one-forms on one surface. We construct the analytic part of that input using theta series attached to auxiliary Hermitian spaces of dimensions one and two, called multiplicity spaces. For a multiplicity line, a component of the first Taylor tensor is a holomorphic one-form. Varying the finite theta input makes its values span a cotangent fiber, so two such constructions give a nonzero wedge. For their product input, the second Taylor tensor for the orthogonal sum of the two lines has a double-alternating projection that is a nonzero multiple of that wedge. This total two-form allows us to change the two multiplicity lines while keeping their sum and its finite theta input fixed.

<a id="eq:theta-mixed-period-goal"></a>
For the two sign pairs in the normalized switch, we will obtain total two-forms $\xi,\xi'$ on one fixed compact ball quotient $X$. On a common principal cover $\pi:X'\to X$, they have finite expansions into holomorphic one-forms: $$\pi^*\xi=\sum_r a_r\Theta_1(\phi_{1,r})\wedge\Theta_2(\phi_{2,r}),
 \qquad
 \pi^*\xi'=\sum_s b_s\Theta_3(\phi_{3,s})\wedge\Theta_4(\phi_{4,s})$$ such that $$\label{eq:theta-mixed-period-goal}
 \int_{X'} \pi^*\xi\wedge\overline{\pi^*\xi'}\ne0.$$ The comparison of $\xi'$ with $\xi$ keeps the rational symplectic space, the rational special-unitary group, the total finite theta input, and the compact quotient fixed. Only afterward will we factor the rational endpoints and pass to $X'$, on which the individual one-forms in both sums descend. At this stage they give complex cohomology classes. Section <a href="#sec:arithmetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arithmetic">5</a> will provide projective principal levels, and Section <a href="#sec:cm-sources" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cm-sources">6</a> will identify the rational CM source maps.

<a id="subsec:theta-ball"></a>
#### The ball and the sign data

<a id="eq:theta-sign-inversion"></a>
Let $E\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}$ be a Galois CM field, put $F=E^+$, and assume $s=[F:\mathbb{Q}]\ge2$. Write $G_E=\mathop{\mathrm{Gal}}(E/\mathbb{Q})$ and let $c\in G_E$ be complex conjugation. Fix a set $\Sigma\subset G_E$ of representatives modulo $c$, including the distinguished embedding $\mu=1$. The construction for a single multiplicity line takes an odd sign function $v:G_E\to\{1,-1\}$ with $v(1)=1$. We index its signs by $$\label{eq:theta-sign-inversion}
 d(\tau)=v(\tau^{-1}),\qquad
 d(c\tau)=-d(\tau),\qquad d(\mu)=1.$$ Thus $d(g^{-1})=v(g)$, the indexing needed by the arithmetic calculation. For the normalized switch, fix four such functions with $d_1+d_2=d_3+d_4$. We first construct the one-form for an arbitrary one of them, and then compare the pairs $(d_1,d_2)$ and $(d_3,d_4)$. All Hermitian forms in this section are linear in their first argument.

<a id="eq:theta-cotangent"></a>
Choose an $E/F$-Hermitian space $V$ of $E$-dimension three, of signature $(2,1)$ at $\mu$ and positive definite at every other real place of $F$. For example, take a diagonal form with two totally positive coefficients and a third coefficient negative at $\mu$ and positive elsewhere; weak approximation in $F$ supplies the last coefficient. Put $$G=\mathop{\mathrm{SU}}(V),\qquad
 D=\{\,L\subset V_\mu:\dim_\mathbb{C}L=1,\ h_V|_L<0\,\}.$$ The group $G$ is defined over $F$, and $D$ is the complex two-ball. We also write $L$ for the tautological negative line bundle over $D$, and set $Q=\underline{V_\mu}/L$. The Grassmannian tangent calculation gives $$\label{eq:theta-cotangent}
 T_D=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(L,Q),\qquad \Omega_D^1=L\otimes Q^*.$$

Fix an $\mathcal O_E$-lattice $\Lambda\subset V$; all principal congruence levels below are matrix levels for this lattice. Their quotients of $D$ are compact. To see the relevant anisotropy, an isotropic vector defined over $E$ would still be isotropic at a definite real completion, which is impossible. Equivalently, $G$ has no nontrivial $F$-split torus: a nonzero weight space of such a torus in $V$ would be isotropic, since invariance of $h_V$ forces $h_V(x,x)=0$ for a vector of nontrivial split weight. Thus $G$ is $F$-anisotropic. The arithmetic lattice and compactness theorems therefore make its arithmetic subgroups cocompact lattices in $G(F\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{R})$ (Borel and Harish-Chandra 1962) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BorelHarishChandra1962](#ref-BorelHarishChandra1962)]; see also the formulation in (Milne 2005, Theorem 3.3 and Proposition 3.5) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[MilneShimura2017](#ref-MilneShimura2017)]. Here $$G(F\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{R})\simeq \mathop{\mathrm{SU}}(2,1)\times\prod_{\tau\ne\mu}\mathop{\mathrm{SU}}(3).$$ Projection past the compact factors preserves discreteness and cocompactness. Indeed, a sequence in an arithmetic subgroup whose active projections approach the identity has, after taking a subsequence, convergent compact components; differences would then approach the identity in the full discrete subgroup. A sufficiently deep principal matrix level is torsion-free. Its action on $D$ is free, because a discrete subgroup has finite intersection with a point stabilizer. Hence its quotient is a smooth connected compact Kähler surface. Later we will impose a principal level for which the reference moduli construction also proves projectivity.

<a id="subsec:theta-lagrangians"></a>
#### Positive Lagrangians and differential bundles

<a id="eq:theta-symplectic-form"></a>
Fix $\delta\in E^\times$ with $c(\delta)=-\delta$. For a nondegenerate $E/F$-Hermitian space $P$ of $E$-dimension $n\in\{1,2\}$, let $$\mathbb V_P=\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{E/F}(V\otimes_E P).$$ It is an $F$-space of dimension $6n$. The product of the two Hermitian forms is a well-defined Hermitian form on $V\otimes_E P$, and we use the $F$-valued form $$\label{eq:theta-symplectic-form}
 \psi_P(v\otimes p,v'\otimes p')
  =\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{E/F}\!\bigl(\delta\,h_V(v,v')h_P(p,p')\bigr).$$ Interchanging the arguments conjugates the Hermitian product and changes the sign of $\delta$, so $\psi_P$ is alternating. It is nondegenerate: the tensor-product Hermitian form is nondegenerate, and the trace pairing on $E/F$ is nondegenerate. More explicitly, if the Hermitian pairing of $x\ne0$ with some $y$ is $b\ne0$, then its pairing with $e y$, for $e\in E$, is $c(e)b$; trace duality supplies $e$ for which $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{E/F}(\delta c(e)b)\ne0$. Thus $(\mathbb V_P,\psi_P)$ is a symplectic space over $F$. Its underlying rational vector space carries the alternating form $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}\psi_P$; this is the restriction of scalars used in complexifications over $\mathbb{Q}$ below. Symplectic coordinates and transformations will still be taken over $F$.

First take $P=W_i$ to be a Hermitian line associated with a sign function $d_i$ as in <a href="#eq:theta-sign-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-sign-inversion">[eq:theta-sign-inversion]</a>. Complexifying the trace pairing identifies the $c\tau$ summand complex-linearly with the dual of the $\tau$ summand. Define a subbundle $\mathcal F_i\subset\mathbb V_P\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{C}$ over $D$ at the active pair of embeddings by <a id="eq:theta-active-lagrangian"></a>$$\label{eq:theta-active-lagrangian}
 (\mathcal F_i)_\mu=L\otimes(W_i)_\mu,\qquad
 (\mathcal F_i)_{c\mu}=Q^*\otimes(W_i)_\mu^*.$$ Here $Q^*=\operatorname{ann}(L)\subset V_\mu^*$, embedded in the conjugate summand by the Hermitian pairing. It is the holomorphic annihilator bundle, not an antiholomorphically varying orthogonal complement in $V_\mu$. At $\tau\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mu\}$, take the full $c\tau$ summand when $d_i(\tau)=1$, and the full $\tau$ summand when $d_i(\tau)=-1$; take zero on the other summand. For a rational orthogonal sum $P=W_1\perp W_2$, take the direct sum of these prescriptions and write $\mathcal F_P=\mathcal F_1\oplus\mathcal F_2$.

<a id="stmt-lem-theta-positive-lagrangian"></a>
<div id="lem:theta-positive-lagrangian" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:theta-halfdet"></a>
**Lemma 11**. *The Hermitian forms on the lines $W_i$ can be chosen over $E/F$ so that $\mathcal F_P$ is a holomorphic positive Lagrangian for $\psi_P$, equivariantly for $G(F\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{R})$. Its determinant and an equivariant square root have the form $$\label{eq:theta-halfdet}
 \det\mathcal F_P\simeq L^{2n}\otimes C_P,\qquad
 (\det\mathcal F_P)^{1/2}\simeq L^n\otimes C_P^{1/2},$$ where $C_P$ is a constant complex line on which $G(F\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{R})$ acts trivially.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-theta-positive-lagrangian"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-theta-positive-lagrangian</summary>

*Proof.* Write $\tau(\delta)=it_\tau$, where $t_\tau\in\mathbb{R}^\times$. At the real place represented by $\tau\in\Sigma$, write $\psi_{P,\tau}$ for the complex bilinear extension of the real-place form. Our positivity convention is $$\psi_{P,\tau}(u,\overline u)/i>0
 \qquad\bigl(0\ne u\in
       (\mathcal F_P)_\tau\oplus(\mathcal F_P)_{c\tau}\bigr).$$ On the $\tau$ summand its sign is the sign of $t_\tau h_Vh_P$; on the $c\tau$ summand the sign is the opposite. At $\mu$, choose the coefficient of each line form to have sign $-\operatorname{sgn}(t_\mu)$. The negative line $L$ on the $\mu$ side and the dual of the positive plane on the $c\mu$ side then both have positive sign. At a definite place $\tau\ne\mu$, choose the line coefficient to have sign $$-d_i(\tau)\operatorname{sgn}(t_\tau).$$ The chosen full summand has positive sign in either case. These finitely many strict sign conditions on an element of $F$ are simultaneously realizable by weak approximation.

At the active pair the two subspaces are annihilators under the complexified pairing; at every other pair one side is full and the other zero. Thus $\mathcal F_P$ is isotropic of half the total dimension. The active annihilator description proves holomorphy, and the compact-place summands are constant over $D$. Equivariance follows from the action on $L$ and its annihilator at $\mu$, and from preservation of the full embedding summands at the compact places.

Finally $$\det Q^*=L\otimes\det(V_\mu)^*.$$ For general $P$ the active determinant is therefore $$\det(L\otimes P_\mu)\otimes\det(Q^*\otimes P_\mu^*)
 \simeq
 L^{2n}\otimes\bigl(\det(V_\mu)^*\bigr)^{\otimes n}
       \otimes\det(P_\mu)^*.$$ All factors other than $L^{2n}$, including the determinants at the compact places, are constant lines. Their special-unitary actions are trivial, because they involve powers of determinants of $V_\tau$ or $V_\tau^*$, while $G$ acts trivially on $P$. This proves the first isomorphism in <a href="#eq:theta-halfdet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-halfdet">[eq:theta-halfdet]</a>; choosing a square root of the constant line gives the second. ◻

</details>

We choose nonzero complex vectors in these constant lines when identifying tensors with differential forms. Such choices only rescale those forms by nonzero constants. No rational trivialization is asserted. Also, the constant lines are trivial for $G=\mathop{\mathrm{SU}}(V)$; they need not be trivial under the larger unitary group.

<a id="eq:theta-one-target"></a>
<a id="eq:theta-top-target"></a>
The determinant calculation gives the differential targets for the theta tensors. For a multiplicity line $W_i$, the relevant target is $$\label{eq:theta-one-target}
\begin{aligned}
 (\det\mathcal F_i)^{1/2}\otimes(\mathcal F_i)_{c\mu}
 &\simeq L\otimes Q^*\otimes(\text{constant line})\\
 &\simeq \Omega_D^1\otimes(\text{constant line}).
\end{aligned}$$ For $W=W_1\perp W_2$, define the double-alternating map $$\operatorname{alt}_2:
 \mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2(Q^*\otimes W_\mu^*)\longrightarrow
       \Lambda^2Q^*\otimes\Lambda^2W_\mu^*$$ by $$\operatorname{alt}_2\bigl((a\otimes u)(b\otimes v)\bigr)
       =(a\wedge b)\otimes(u\wedge v).$$ Here the product on the left is multiplication in the symmetric algebra. Exchanging its two factors changes both exterior signs, so the map is well-defined. Including the half-determinant line gives $$\label{eq:theta-top-target}
 (\det\mathcal F_W)^{1/2}\otimes
       \Lambda^2Q^*\otimes\Lambda^2W_\mu^*
       \simeq K_D\otimes(\text{constant line}),$$ because $K_D=\det\Omega_D^1=L^2\otimes\Lambda^2Q^*$. The first theta tensor will give the one-form in <a href="#eq:theta-one-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-one-target">[eq:theta-one-target]</a>; the double-alternating part of the second will give the two-form in <a href="#eq:theta-top-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-top-target">[eq:theta-top-target]</a>.

The reason to combine the two lines is geometric. At the active pair, their total positive Lagrangian has the pieces $$L\otimes W_\mu,\qquad Q^*\otimes W_\mu^*.$$ These depend only on $W_\mu$, independently of its decomposition into lines. At a compact place, let $W_\tau^+$ and $W_\tau^-$ be the sums of the multiplicity lines with respective signs $+1$ and $-1$ under $d_i$. The total positive pieces there are $$V_\tau\otimes W_\tau^-,\qquad
 V_\tau^*\otimes(W_\tau^+)^*;$$ the second dual is embedded as $\operatorname{ann}(W_\tau^-)\subset W_\tau^*$. The relation $d_1+d_2=d_3+d_4$ says that the two sign pairs have the same multiset at each place. Thus the original local lines can be relabeled with signs $d_3,d_4$ without changing these total pieces. Weak approximation will produce a rational decomposition with the new signs and nearby compact-place data, while leaving the active total Lagrangian unchanged. We will apply this approximation in Subsection <a href="#subsec:theta-approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="subsec:theta-approximation">4.7</a> to one fixed theta input. To compare the resulting forms on a compact quotient, we first establish their covariance and congruence descent.

<a id="subsec:theta-covariance"></a>
#### Rational theta covariance

Choose symplectic coordinates over $F$ on $\mathbb V_P$, written $(p,q)\in F^a\oplus F^a$, where $a=3n$, with $$\psi_P((p,q),(p',q'))=p^tq'-q^tp'.$$ The Schrödinger position coordinates are $q$. A positive Lagrangian at a real place has a unique graph frame $$e_Z=\binom ZI,\qquad Z=Z^t,\qquad \operatorname{Im}Z>0.$$ Indeed, it cannot meet the $p$-axis nontrivially, because $\psi_P(u,\bar u)$ vanishes there. Projection to the $q$-axis is therefore an isomorphism. Isotropy says $Z^t=Z$, and, for a graph vector, $$\psi_{P,\tau}(e_Zu,\overline{e_Zu})/i
       =2u^t(\operatorname{Im}Z)\bar u,$$ which proves the last condition. The positive Lagrangians above thus give a period map $D\to\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma}\mathfrak H_a$.

<a id="eq:theta-completion"></a>
Let $\phi\in\mathcal S(\mathbb{A}_{F,f}^a)$ be a finite-adelic Schwartz–Bruhat function. For $Z=(Z_\tau)_\tau\in\prod_\tau\mathfrak H_a$ and $z=(z_\tau)_\tau\in\bigoplus_\tau\mathbb{C}^a$, put <a id="eq:theta-series"></a>$$\label{eq:theta-series}
 \vartheta_\phi(Z,z)
  =\sum_{x\in F^a}\phi(x)
    \exp\!\left(
       \pi i\sum_{\tau\in\Sigma}x_\tau^tZ_\tau x_\tau+
       2\pi i\sum_{\tau\in\Sigma}x_\tau^tz_\tau\right).$$ The rational transformation law for this series includes an exponential quadratic in $z$, which mixes its ordinary Taylor coefficients. The following completion cancels that factor and gives coefficients with a tensor transformation law. Define $$\label{eq:theta-completion}
 \begin{split}
 \widehat\vartheta_\phi(Z,z)
  &=\exp\!\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\sum_{\tau\in\Sigma}
       z_\tau^t(\operatorname{Im}Z_\tau)^{-1}z_\tau\right)
       \vartheta_\phi(Z,z),\\
 \widehat\vartheta_\phi(Z,z)
  &=\sum_{k\ge0}\langle T_{k,\phi}(Z),z^{\otimes k}\rangle .
 \end{split}$$ Thus $T_{k,\phi}$ includes the factor $1/k!$ in the $k$-th derivative. We use multiplication in the symmetric algebra for these homogeneous coefficients.

The series and every fixed order of its derivatives converge uniformly on compact subsets of the Siegel and $z$ domains. Indeed, the rational points in the support of $\phi$ lie in finitely many translates of a full lattice in $(F\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{R})^a$. On a compact set the imaginary parts have a common positive lower bound, and the summands, after a fixed number of differentiations, are bounded by a polynomial in $\|x\|$ times $$C\exp(-c\|x\|^2+C'\|x\|).$$ The lattice sum of this bound converges. The completion and its derivatives obey the same bounds on compact sets. The same conclusion holds after any fixed number of derivatives in smooth parameters on a compact subset of a smooth parameter domain: there the period map and all derivatives of it needed for that order are bounded, and its imaginary parts have a common positive lower bound. Compactness of the image of a period map alone, without bounds on its parameter derivatives, would not imply this last assertion.

We next specify the rational Weil-representation input, including the cover being used. The circle extension is obtained from the metaplectic double cover by extending its central $\{1,-1\}$ to the group of unit complex scalars. Take the global additive character of $\mathbb{A}_F/F$ whose infinite component is $t\mapsto\exp(2\pi i\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{F/\mathbb{Q}}t)$. Weil’s rational section in the adelic circle extension leaves the summation functional $$\lambda(\Phi)=\sum_{x\in F^a}\Phi(x)$$ invariant. In characteristic zero his pseudosymplectic group is the symplectic group, and his adelic twofold subgroup contains this rational section (Weil 1964, Ch. III, nos. 31 and 41, Eq. (39) and Theorem 6; Ch. IV, no. 45, pp. 200–201) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Weil1964](#ref-Weil1964)]. Consequently, for a rational symplectic transformation we may choose an infinite double-cover lift and its paired finite lift whose adelic product is the rational lift. A change of the infinite sign requires the same change of the finite sign. These paired lifts give rational covariance; they are not an assertion that the symplectic double cover splits on an arbitrary adelic unitary group.

<a id="stmt-lem-theta-tensor-law"></a>
<div id="lem:theta-tensor-law" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:theta-jacobi-law"></a>
<a id="eq:theta-taylor-law"></a>
**Lemma 12**. *Let $g\in\mathop{\mathrm{Sp}}(\mathbb V_P)(F)$. At each real place write $$g=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\C&D_0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 J=CZ+D_0,\qquad gZ=(AZ+B)J^{-1}.$$ Choose an infinite metaplectic lift with factor $j(g,Z)$ satisfying $j(g,Z)^2=\prod_{\tau\in\Sigma}\det J_\tau$, and let $\widetilde g_f$ be its paired finite lift. Then $$\label{eq:theta-jacobi-law}
 \vartheta_{\omega_f(\widetilde g_f)\phi}(gZ,J^{-t}z)
  =j(g,Z)\exp\!\left(\pi i\sum_{\tau\in\Sigma}
             z_\tau^tJ_\tau^{-1}C_\tau z_\tau\right)
             \vartheta_\phi(Z,z).$$ The completed coefficients satisfy $$\label{eq:theta-taylor-law}
 T_{k,\omega_f(\widetilde g_f)\phi}(gZ)
   =j(g,Z)\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^k(J)\,T_{k,\phi}(Z).$$ They are thus tensors valued in $$(\det\mathcal F_P)^{1/2}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^k\mathcal F_P.$$ In particular the positive Lagrangian, not its dual, occurs in the symmetric factor. The tensor $T_{1,\phi}$ is holomorphic.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-theta-tensor-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-theta-tensor-law</summary>

<a id="eq:theta-gaussian-law"></a>
*Proof.* We first check the infinite formula, keeping track of its signs. At one real place write $$f_{Z,z}(x)=\exp(\pi ix^tZx+2\pi ix^tz),\qquad K(g,Z)=J^{-1}C.$$ The matrix $K(g,Z)$ is symmetric; this follows from $JC^t=CJ^t$, a symplectic identity. The formula to prove is $$\label{eq:theta-gaussian-law}
 \omega_\infty(\widetilde g)f_{Z,z}
  =j(g,Z)^{-1}\exp(-\pi iz^tK(g,Z)z)
       f_{gZ,J^{-t}z}.$$ For symmetric $B$, the upper unipotent $n(B)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}I&B\\0&I\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ acts by multiplication by $\exp(\pi ix^tBx)$, which gives <a href="#eq:theta-gaussian-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-gaussian-law">[eq:theta-gaussian-law]</a> with $j=1$. For $m(A)=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(A,A^{-t})$, the chosen lift acts by $$\omega_\infty(\widetilde m(A))b(x)
       =j(m(A))^{-1}b(A^tx),\qquad
 j(m(A))^2=(\det A)^{-1}.$$ For $\det A>0$ the positive choice has $j(m(A))^{-1}=|\det A|^{1/2}$. For negative determinant the metaplectic phase is retained in $j$. Since $m(A)Z=AZA^t$ and $J^{-t}z=Az$, this is the required formula.

For $w=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-I\\I&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, choose the lift $$b\longmapsto e^{-\pi ia/4}
        \int_{\mathbb{R}^a}b(y)e^{-2\pi ix^ty}\,dy.$$ The Gaussian integral is $$\int_{\mathbb{R}^a}f_{Z,z}(y)e^{-2\pi ix^ty}\,dy
  =\det(-iZ)^{-1/2}
       \exp\!\bigl(-\pi i(x-z)^tZ^{-1}(x-z)\bigr),$$ where the square root of $\det(-iZ)$ equals $1$ at $Z=iI$. Taking $$j(w,Z)=e^{\pi ia/4}\det(-iZ)^{1/2}$$ gives $j(w,Z)^2=\det Z$ and proves <a href="#eq:theta-gaussian-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-gaussian-law">[eq:theta-gaussian-law]</a> for $w$.

These generators give the formula for the whole real symplectic group. To check that the displayed exponent also composes correctly, let $J_2=J(g_2,Z)$ and $J_1=J(g_1,g_2Z)$. The symplectic identities give $$J(g_1g_2,Z)=J_1J_2,\qquad
 K(g_1g_2,Z)=K(g_2,Z)+J_2^{-1}K(g_1,g_2Z)J_2^{-t}.$$ For the second identity, use $A_2-(g_2Z)C_2=J_2^{-t}$ to write $C_{12}=C_1J_2^{-t}+J_1C_2$, and multiply by $(J_1J_2)^{-1}$. Together with the metaplectic group law for $j$, this is exactly the composition law in <a href="#eq:theta-gaussian-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-gaussian-law">[eq:theta-gaussian-law]</a>. Multiplying the formula over the real places gives its infinite adelic version.

Apply rational invariance of $\lambda$ to $\phi\otimes\prod_\tau f_{Z_\tau,z_\tau}$. By <a href="#eq:theta-gaussian-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-gaussian-law">[eq:theta-gaussian-law]</a>, the transformed sum is $$j(g,Z)^{-1}
 \exp\!\left(-\pi i\sum_\tau z_\tau^tJ_\tau^{-1}C_\tau z_\tau\right)
 \vartheta_{\omega_f(\widetilde g_f)\phi}(gZ,J^{-t}z).$$ Equating this with the original sum gives <a href="#eq:theta-jacobi-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-jacobi-law">[eq:theta-jacobi-law]</a>. In particular the finite action on the left is $\omega_f(\widetilde g_f)$, with no inverse. If one instead keeps the finite input on the left fixed, the input on the right is acted on by $\omega_f(\widetilde g_f)^{-1}$.

For the completion, put $Y=\operatorname{Im}Z$ and $Y'=\operatorname{Im}(gZ)$ at one place. From $Y'=\bar J^{-t}YJ^{-1}$, and then $\bar J^t=J^t-2iYC^t$, we obtain <a id="eq:theta-completion-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:theta-completion-identity}
 J^{-1}(Y')^{-1}J^{-t}=Y^{-1}-2iJ^{-1}C.$$ Here $C^tJ^{-t}=J^{-1}C$ by symmetry. Thus the completion factor at $(gZ,J^{-t}z)$ differs from that at $(Z,z)$ by $\exp(-\pi iz^tJ^{-1}Cz)$. This cancels the exponential in <a href="#eq:theta-jacobi-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-jacobi-law">[eq:theta-jacobi-law]</a>, so $$\widehat\vartheta_{\omega_f(\widetilde g_f)\phi}(gZ,J^{-t}z)
       =j(g,Z)\widehat\vartheta_\phi(Z,z).$$ Comparison of homogeneous terms yields <a href="#eq:theta-taylor-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-taylor-law">[eq:theta-taylor-law]</a>. Finally, $$g e_Z=e_{gZ}J.$$ Hence $J$ is the transformation matrix for vectors in the positive Lagrangian; this proves the asserted tensor target and its orientation. The completion has no linear term in $z$, so $T_{1,\phi}$ is the ordinary holomorphic theta gradient. For comparison, the first-order law agrees with the classical theta-gradient formula in (Möller and Zagier 2016, sec. 9.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[MollerZagier2016](#ref-MollerZagier2016)]. ◻

</details>

For a multiplicity line $W_i$, the $c\mu$-projection of $T_{1,\phi}$ therefore lies in the cotangent target <a href="#eq:theta-one-target" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-one-target">[eq:theta-one-target]</a>. Rational covariance alone does not supply a congruence subgroup fixing a given finite input. Subsection <a href="#subsec:theta-splittings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="subsec:theta-splittings">4.4</a> obtains such stabilizers from an honest smooth finite-adelic action.

<a id="stmt-rem-theta-adapted-coordinates"></a>
<div id="rem:theta-adapted-coordinates" class="remark">

**Remark 13**. We will use the lemma to change rational Schrödinger coordinates. Suppose a rational symplectic matrix $S$ takes adapted coordinate vectors to the reference coordinate vectors. Then $Z_{\mathrm{ref}}=S Z_{\mathrm{ad}}$, and the input in the adapted coordinates is $$\phi_{\mathrm{ad}}
    =\omega_f(\widetilde S_f)^{-1}\phi_{\mathrm{ref}}.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:theta-taylor-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-taylor-law">[eq:theta-taylor-law]</a> compares the resulting tensors. Changing the sign of the paired lift changes both the infinite factor and the finite input sign, so their comparison is unchanged. For coordinates adapted to a rational symplectic direct sum, the period matrix is block diagonal and the completed theta series of a product input is the product of the two completed series.

</div>

<a id="subsec:theta-splittings"></a>
#### Unitary splittings and congruence descent

The preceding lemma uses paired lifts of rational symplectic elements. An honest action on finite inputs, and therefore a congruence stabilizer for each input, requires a different statement. We use the unitary splitting theorem in the following precise form. For a quadratic extension $E/F$, a Hermitian space $H$ of dimension $m$, a skew-Hermitian space $K$ of dimension $n$, and a global additive character, choose unitary Hecke characters $\chi_H,\chi_K$ of $E$ with $$\chi_H|_{\mathbb{A}_F^\times/F^\times}=\epsilon_{E/F}^{\,m},\qquad
 \chi_K|_{\mathbb{A}_F^\times/F^\times}=\epsilon_{E/F}^{\,n}.$$ The corresponding unitary actions lift to the *circle-scalar extension* of the adelic symplectic Weil representation. The global lift is assembled from local lifts and agrees with the canonical rational symplectic section on rational unitary points. Its finite part acts smoothly on finite Schwartz functions. Changing the splitting characters twists the unitary actions by characters composed with their determinants. Here $\epsilon_{E/F}$ is the quadratic idele class character.

The local splitting is due to Kudla (Kudla 1994) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Kudla1994](#ref-Kudla1994)]. We use the circle-extension formulation and its local and adelic compatibility in (Borade et al. 2025, secs. 3.1–3.1.1 and 4.1–4.1.1) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[BoradeEtAl2025ToriPeriods](#ref-BoradeEtAl2025ToriPeriods)]. In the present CM setting, adelic automorphy and the determinant-twist rule are also recorded in (Bergeron, Millson, and Mœglin 2016, sec. 7.2 and Remark 7.3) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[BMM2016](#ref-BMM2016)]. Throughout this invocation the extension is the circle pushout: we do not assert that the double cover splits on every unitary group. We will compare this circle lift with an actual double lift only after restriction to $G=\mathop{\mathrm{SU}}(V)$. The split-place normalization below will also enter the local Hecke calculation of Subsection <a href="#subsec:active-parameter" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="subsec:active-parameter">6.1</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-unitary-splitting"></a>
<div id="lem:unitary-splitting" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *For the symplectic space $(\mathbb V_P,\psi_P)$, restriction of the unitary splitting to $G(\mathbb{A}_F)$ yields an honest finite-adelic Weil action $\omega_{P,f}$. It is compatible with the rational covariance in Lemma <a href="#lem:theta-tensor-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:theta-tensor-law">12</a> and is independent of the splitting characters. At infinity its half-determinant action is the natural action on $L^n$ in <a href="#eq:theta-halfdet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-halfdet">[eq:theta-halfdet]</a>.*

<a id="eq:theta-linear-action"></a>
*At a split finite place, choose as Schrödinger positions for a multiplicity line one standard three-dimensional embedding summand. The resulting $\mathop{\mathrm{SL}}_3$-action is inverse linear substitution. More generally, the normalized linear action associated with a transformation $h$ of the position space is $$\label{eq:theta-linear-action}
 (\rho(h)b)(x)=|\det h|^{-1/2}b(h^{-1}x).$$ A bare metaplectic lift outside $\mathop{\mathrm{SL}}_3$ is the normalized operator $\rho(h)$ times its lift scalar; that scalar must be retained in any use of such a lift.*

*Every finite list of inputs is fixed under $\omega_{P,f}$ by all sufficiently deep principal lattice congruence subgroups.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-unitary-splitting"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-unitary-splitting</summary>

*Proof.* First we check that the required splitting characters exist. For the idele class groups $C_F$ and $C_E$, the natural map $C_F\to C_E$ is injective: if an $F$-idele becomes a principal $E$-idele, its generator is fixed by $c$ and hence belongs to $F$. This map is also proper. The absolute idele norm on $C_E$ restricts to the square of the norm on $C_F$, whose norm-one kernel is compact. A compact subset of $C_E$ has norms in a compact subinterval of $\mathbb{R}_{>0}$; its preimage is therefore a closed subset of a compact norm interval times the compact norm-one kernel of $C_F$. Thus $C_F$ is a closed locally compact subgroup of $C_E$. The character-extension theorem for a closed subgroup of a locally compact abelian group extends each unitary character $\epsilon_{E/F}^{\,r}$ from $C_F$ to $C_E$ (Folland 2015, sec. 4.3, Corollary 4.42) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Folland2015](#ref-Folland2015)]. Apply this with $r=m,n$.

The splitting theorem applies to our form by replacing $h_P$ with the skew-Hermitian form $\delta h_P$. With both forms linear in the first variable, the trace product in (Borade et al. 2025, sec. 2.1 and 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[BoradeEtAl2025ToriPeriods](#ref-BoradeEtAl2025ToriPeriods)] is exactly <a href="#eq:theta-symplectic-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-symplectic-form">[eq:theta-symplectic-form]</a>. We take the same global additive character as in Lemma <a href="#lem:theta-tensor-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:theta-tensor-law">12</a>. The determinant twists from changing the characters vanish on $G=\mathop{\mathrm{SU}}(V)$. The product of the finite local splittings is therefore an honest action $\omega_{P,f}$ on $G(\mathbb{A}_{F,f})$, independent of those characters.

We compare its infinite circle lift with the double lift needed for the differential bundles. At the active maximal compact subgroup $S(\mathop{\mathrm{U}}(2)\times\mathop{\mathrm{U}}(1))$, let $\ell$ be the character on the negative line. The determinant of the positive plane is $\ell^{-1}$, so the determinant character on $\mathcal F_P$ is $\ell^{2n}$. At every other real place the determinant character is trivial on $\mathop{\mathrm{SU}}(3)$. On the maximal compact $\mathop{\mathrm{U}}(a)$ of each real symplectic factor, the metaplectic double cover is the determinant-square-root cover. For the product of the real factors its square is the product of those determinant characters. Its pullback to the maximal compact subgroup of $G(F\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{R})$ therefore splits with square-root character $\ell^n$.

A connected real reductive group retracts onto its maximal compact subgroup. The preceding determinant computation says that every fundamental-group loop of $G(F\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{R})$ has even determinant winding in the symplectic group. The lift from the universal covering group consequently descends to a group lift of $G(F\otimes_\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{R})$ into the double cover. Its half-determinant action is the one on $L^n$ in <a href="#eq:theta-halfdet" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-halfdet">[eq:theta-halfdet]</a>.
